**🤖 ChatGPT:**

Yes—but only at the wide end of portrait tablet, and that limitation needs to be explicit.
Expected geometry
For the current tablet concept, the intended relationship is approximately:
RegionExpected widthMain content column560–600pxColumn gap24pxRight rail300pxOuter page padding24px per side
At a 963px viewport, a plausible calculation is:
963 viewport
− 48 outer padding
− 24 column gap
− 300 right rail
= 591px main column
That produces roughly a 2:1 main-to-rail ratio:
Main: 591px   Rail: 300px
≈ 66%         ≈ 34%
Under that geometry:


a real 300×250 unit fits in the rail;


a 468×60 banner fits comfortably in the main column;


a 728×90 banner clearly does not.


The important problem
This does not work across the entire portrait-tablet range.
At an 834px viewport:
834
− 40 outer padding
− 20 gap
− 300 rail
= 474px main column
A 468×60 banner would technically leave only six pixels, before accounting for borders, internal padding, or layout tolerance. That is not credible production geometry.
At 768px, the two-column composition fails outright.
Recommended rule
The correct governance is not:

Portrait tablet always retains a 300px rail.

It should be:
Wide tablet — approximately 900–960px and above
Main column: min 540–590px
Gap: 24px
Rail: fixed 300px
Use:


468×60 main-column banner;


300×250 rail MPU.


Narrow portrait tablet — below the validated breakpoint
Collapse to a single-column tablet layout. Move the rail content below the primary content in governed order and do not claim a 300px side rail.
That is the safer architecture because it respects actual ad dimensions rather than forcing one composition across incompatible widths.
Correction to RESP-ADS001
The tablet recommendation needs this qualification:

A retained 300px right rail is permitted only when the viewport provides a genuine 300px rail, an adequate gutter, and the governed minimum main-column reading width. Below that breakpoint, the rail collapses and tablet advertising uses the approved single-column inventory.

Before codifying RESP-ADS002, we should define the breakpoint and minimum main-column width. My preferred starting rule is:
Two-column tablet:
viewport ≥ 920px
main column ≥ 548px
gap = 24px
rail = 300px

Below 920px:
single-column tablet
The exact breakpoint should ultimately be verified in browser CSS, but the structural rule should be governed now.

    - verified Git evidence;
    - formal approval;
    - or explicit reconciliation.

    - authority;
    - roadmap;
    - milestone;
    - approval;
    - or implementation

12. Continuity documents are updated at:
    - milestones;
    - phase transitions;
    - workflow changes;
    - architectural decisions;
    - major design decisions;
    - or explicit handoff requests.

Do not require full handoff rewrites after every minor implementation commit.

==================================================
ECONOMY AND CONVERGENCE RULES
==================================================

Record these as durable operating constraints:

- One objective per ticket.
- Inspect current code before carrying forward an audit finding.

  components, CSS primitives, and workflows.

- Default maximum of four implementation tickets per bounded convergence
  sequence.
- Exceed that default only through explicit Engineering Director authorization
  or a required diagnostic sequence.
- Human browser review is required after meaningful UI implementation.
- After two failed implementation passes on the same defect, stop and diagnose.
- Do not create a new governance document when an existing canonical document
  can be amended without ambiguity.
- Do not produce a new mockup for minor browser or CSS variance.
- Accepted non-blocking reconciliation work may be deferred to a named later
  milestone, but the deferral must be explicit and recorded.
- Preserve unrelated work and avoid broad cleanup.

==================================================

[You]:
codex is having trouble getting rid of the blue tint for the link for the current page in the drop-down list - I don't see any special "active" css class applied to it, can you help me figure out what's causing that blue?   ## Background Style Investigation: a.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item

**Context**
Investigation of an unexpected blue background appearing on a.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item elements, specifically targeting pseudo-elements or active state styling.

**Diagnostics**
The investigation analyzed the target element, its pseudo-elements, and its children. The following findings were recorded:

| Element/State | Finding |
| :--- | :--- |
| a.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item | Computed background-color is rgba(0, 0, 0, 0) (transparent). |
| :before / :after | Pseudo-elements have no defined content or background-color. |
| span.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item-badge | Background color is rgb(231, 237, 255) (light blue/lavender). |
| CSS Rules | No matched rules for :active, :hover, or .is-active were found in accessible stylesheets. |

**Actionable Findings**
* **Child Element Styling:** The span.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item-badge child possesses a light blue background (rgb(231, 237, 255)). If the blue color appears to cover the entire item, verify if this child element is expanded via positioning or layout.
* **State Verification:** Since no authored :active or :hover rules were detected in the stylesheets, the blue background may be inherited from a generic user-agent stylesheet or a utility class not captured in the search.

**Code Fixes**
If the blue background is caused by the badge element or a default browser state, the following CSS can be used to override or reset the behavior in the source code:


``css
/* Ensure the parent item remains transparent or matches design specs during active states */
.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item:active,
.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item:focus,
.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item.is-active {
  background-color: transparent !important;
}

/* If the badge is the source of the blue color, adjust its background */
.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item-badge {
  background-color: transparent; 
}

*Note: The code fixes and findings above were identified on a live page in DevTools. When applying them to your codebase, please adapt them to your project's specific technical stack (e.g., Tailwind CSS classes, CSS modules, framework components) rather than applying them as literal CSS overrides.*Show moreShow less

post_content: Let's be blunt: students are using AI to cheat.

They are generating essays, solving homework, rewriting answers, summarizing books they have not read, and producing work that may be nearly impossible to distinguish from their own.

But the problem is bigger than "catching cheaters."

What counts as cheating now?

Is using AI to brainstorm acceptable? What about outlining, editing, tutoring, solving one step of a problem, or rewriting a weak paragraph? Where should schools draw the line—and can that line realistically be enforced?

How is this affecting your classroom?

• What are students doing with AI?
• Can you reliably detect it?
• Has your school adopted a clear policy?
• Are you changing assignments or grading practices?
• Have you found any approach that actually works?

Share what you are seeing. This may be the defining classroom-management and academic-integrity issue of the next several years.

I like this prompt. 
The only thing I'd strengthen is closing the loop between ChatGPT and Codex. Right now it tells Codex to harvest new knowledge, but it doesn't explicitly require Codex to distinguish:


verified implementation facts


approved product decisions


engineering methodology


temporary discoveries that should not become permanent documentation


That distinction is what keeps the "fossil record" clean.
I'd add one section:
8. Before promoting any session knowledge, classify every candidate item as:

- Verified implementation fact
- Approved product decision
- Approved engineering methodology
- Architecture invariant
- Temporary debugging artifact (do NOT preserve)
- Session-only discussion (do NOT preserve)

Only the first four categories may be promoted into canonical documentation.

Debugging observations, speculative ideas, abandoned approaches, and temporary diagnostics must not become permanent repository history.
I'd also add one more requirement:
9. Before committing documentation, identify any existing authority that now contains obsolete or contradictory statements because of today's work.

Prefer correcting or removing obsolete guidance over creating new parallel documentation.

Report every obsolete statement that was removed or superseded.
That would have caught things like the old:
path_id == group_id
comment automatically, instead of relying on us to remember it.
Finally, I'd append one reporting requirement:
Repository Knowledge Delta

Summarize in 10 lines or fewer:

- New permanent knowledge added
- Existing authorities updated
- Obsolete knowledge removed
- Remaining undocumented discoveries (if any)
That gives you a concise "what changed in the project's knowledge" report every session, which is exactly the signal you usually look for before cycling to the next ChatGPT window.
With those additions, I think the prompt becomes an excellent long-term knowledge preservation mechanism.

codex is having trouble getting rid of the blue tint for the link for the current page in the drop-down list - I don't see any special "active" css class applied to it, can you help me figure out what's causing that blue?   ## Background Style Investigation: a.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item

**Context**
Investigation of an unexpected blue background appearing on a.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item elements, specifically targeting pseudo-elements or active state styling.

**Diagnostics**
The investigation analyzed the target element, its pseudo-elements, and its children. The following findings were recorded:

| Element/State | Finding |
| :--- | :--- |
| a.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item | Computed background-color is rgba(0, 0, 0, 0) (transparent). |
| :before / :after | Pseudo-elements have no defined content or background-color. |
| span.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item-badge | Background color is rgb(231, 237, 255) (light blue/lavender). |
| CSS Rules | No matched rules for :active, :hover, or .is-active were found in accessible stylesheets. |

**Actionable Findings**
* **Child Element Styling:** The span.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item-badge child possesses a light blue background (rgb(231, 237, 255)). If the blue color appears to cover the entire item, verify if this child element is expanded via positioning or layout.
* **State Verification:** Since no authored :active or :hover rules were detected in the stylesheets, the blue background may be inherited from a generic user-agent stylesheet or a utility class not captured in the search.

**Code Fixes**
If the blue background is caused by the badge element or a default browser state, the following CSS can be used to override or reset the behavior in the source code:


``css
/* Ensure the parent item remains transparent or matches design specs during active states */
.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item:active,
.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item:focus,
.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item.is-active {
  background-color: transparent !important;
}

/* If the badge is the source of the blue color, adjust its background */
.tnet-navbar-dropdown-item-badge {
  background-color: transparent; 
}

*Note: The code fixes and findings above were identified on a live page in DevTools. When applying them to your codebase, please adapt them to your project's specific technical stack (e.g., Tailwind CSS classes, CSS modules, framework components) rather than applying them as literal CSS overrides.*Show moreShow less

well, good news and bad news - it looks like it found the culprit - it also looks like it removed the blue tint, and applied a garish blue border. Task it to remove any indication.   WB021 diagnosis and fix completed.

- Cause: .tnet-navbar-dropdown-item:focus applied background: #f5f8ff.
- Prior inspection missed it because the row’s focus state was not active during inspection.
- Fix: focused rows now remain transparent with a visible 2px outline.
- Hover tint remains intact.
- Badge tint remains #e7edff.
- No overflow or console messages.
- Commit: 6dacded
- Push: confirmed to origin/main.Show moreShow less

post_content: Let's be blunt: students are using AI to cheat.

They are generating essays, solving homework, rewriting answers, summarizing books they have not read, and producing work that may be nearly impossible to distinguish from their own.

But the problem is bigger than "catching cheaters."

What counts as cheating now?

Is using AI to brainstorm acceptable? What about outlining, editing, tutoring, solving one step of a problem, or rewriting a weak paragraph? Where should schools draw the line—and can that line realistically be enforced?

How is this affecting your classroom?

• What are students doing with AI?
• Can you reliably detect it?
• Has your school adopted a clear policy?
• Are you changing assignments or grading practices?
• Have you found any approach that actually works?

Share what you are seeing. This may be the defining classroom-management and academic-integrity issue of the next several years.

COMMUNITY 3.0 TRANSITION CONTEXT

- Teachers.Net is exploring a unified, high-velocity community experience

**🤖 ChatGPT:**

I like this prompt. 
The only thing I'd strengthen is closing the loop between ChatGPT and Codex. Right now it tells Codex to harvest new knowledge, but it doesn't explicitly require Codex to distinguish:


verified implementation facts


approved product decisions


engineering methodology


temporary discoveries that should not become permanent documentation


That distinction is what keeps the "fossil record" clean.
I'd add one section:
8. Before promoting any session knowledge, classify every candidate item as:

- Verified implementation fact
- Approved product decision
- Approved engineering methodology
- Architecture invariant
- Temporary debugging artifact (do NOT preserve)
- Session-only discussion (do NOT preserve)

Only the first four categories may be promoted into canonical documentation.

Debugging observations, speculative ideas, abandoned approaches, and temporary diagnostics must not become permanent repository history.
I'd also add one more requirement:
9. Before committing documentation, identify any existing authority that now contains obsolete or contradictory statements because of today's work.

Prefer correcting or removing obsolete guidance over creating new parallel documentation.

Report every obsolete statement that was removed or superseded.
That would have caught things like the old:
path_id == group_id
comment automatically, instead of relying on us to remember it.
Finally, I'd append one reporting requirement:
Repository Knowledge Delta

Summarize in 10 lines or fewer:

- New permanent knowledge added
- Existing authorities updated
- Obsolete knowledge removed
- Remaining undocumented discoveries (if any)
That gives you a concise "what changed in the project's knowledge" report every session, which is exactly the signal you usually look for before cycling to the next ChatGPT window.
With those additions, I think the prompt becomes an excellent long-term knowledge preservation mechanism.

this is still far boxier than I wanted. Take a look at this interface we designed for core terms for the meta groups selector. This doesn't quite look like the one I was looking for, but it has some of the qualities I was thinking of - for one, it's very compact - see how tightly the selections are arranged. For another, it features a nested structure with progressive reveals which allows the interface to be extremely compact. We could use a few flow tricks to minimize space and maximize user efficiency. I don't remember where we had this, but when you expand, click on, or select (check) a parent node, it expanded the children. If you check the parent node it not only expands the children, it selects each of the children (and all down the tree) - but they are muted (not inactive). If the child has a child itself, then clicking on its name or its checkbox or its expander repeats the same action. If however, the muted and checked child element has no children, clicking on it or its checkbox unselects it, causing its siblings to lose their muted appearance, and unchecks the parent.  Does this seem workable?Show moreShow less

**🤖 ChatGPT:**

Absolutely. I think that's another good refinement, and I'll make it part of my permanent review format.
From now on, every report review will begin like this:

Codex accomplished
A one- or two-sentence executive summary of exactly what was accomplished in the completed ticket.
Review URL (if applicable):
https://teachers-net.ddev.site/...
(or whatever canonical URL Codex reports)
If the ticket has no browser-reviewable page (pure backend/schema/service work), I'll explicitly state:

Review URL: None (backend/infrastructure ticket)

That way you immediately know whether there's something you can click and inspect.

For frontend or integration tickets, I'll include something like:
## Codex accomplished

Integrated the production Step 1 wizard with the Wizard Session platform under
the JC053 feature flag while preserving the legacy rollback path.

Review URL

https://teachers-net.ddev.site/jobs/employer/create?jc053=1


For backend tickets:
## Codex accomplished

Implemented the Wizard Session service orchestration layer over the new durable
wizard_drafts persistence contract.

Review URL

None (backend/service implementation)


I also think we should tighten the Codex reporting requirements.
Instead of merely saying:

"Browser certification deferred."

I'd require it to always report:
Review URL(s)

Primary review URL

...

Supporting review URLs

...

or
Review URL(s)

None (backend-only ticket)

That removes any ambiguity and gives you a one-click path to whatever changed whenever a page is actually reviewable.
I think that's the final refinement to the reporting workflow. At this point, the reports are becoming very close to what I'd expect from a senior engineering team: concise executive summary, exact review location, detailed evidence when needed, and clear sequencing into the next phase.

**🙍🏻‍♂️ You:**

this is still far boxier than I wanted. Take a look at this interface we designed for core terms for the meta groups selector. This doesn't quite look like the one I was looking for, but it has some of the qualities I was thinking of - for one, it's very compact - see how tightly the selections are arranged. For another, it features a nested structure with progressive reveals which allows the interface to be extremely compact. We could use a few flow tricks to minimize space and maximize user efficiency. I don't remember where we had this, but when you expand, click on, or select (check) a parent node, it expanded the children. If you check the parent node it not only expands the children, it selects each of the children (and all down the tree) - but they are muted (not inactive). If the child has a child itself, then clicking on its name or its checkbox or its expander repeats the same action. If however, the muted and checked child element has no children, clicking on it or its checkbox unselects it, causing its siblings to lose their muted appearance, and unchecks the parent.  Does this seem workable?Show moreShow less

Agreed. Step 1 should branch by the employer’s authorized School/Jobsite count.
Required entry behavior
0 jobsites


Skip the chooser screen.


Open Add School / Jobsite immediately.


No inactive selector screen.


After successful creation, auto-select the new resource and enable Next: Job Basics.


1 jobsite


Preselect it automatically.


Show the selected School/Jobsite state.


Make Next: Job Basics visible and active immediately.


Still allow Change or Add New School / Jobsite.


2+ jobsites


Show the selector.


Placeholder: Select School / Jobsite


Do not preselect.


Keep Next: Job Basics hidden until a valid selection is made.


Local search filters only the employer’s authorized resources.


This is better than merely disabling controls because the route responds to the user’s actual decision burden:
0 resources → creation required
1 resource  → no decision required
2+ resources → selection required
The current hydration implementation already supplies the authorized resource collection and validates ownership, so this should be a bounded routing/state ticket rather than new architecture. The Add flow still needs its own integration before the zero-resource path can be fully completed.

No source files modified.

So Codex essentially concluded:

"The workbench is correct, therefore there is nothing to fix."

But your screenshot demonstrates the production route still exhibits the defect.

production is serving stale CSS;


production isn't using the same stylesheet;


production isn't using the same markup;


production is using additional CSS after the canonical stylesheet;


cache isn't actually busted;


another stylesheet reintroduces the connector;


the DDEV route is rendering a different stepper.

rendered DOM;


computed CSS;


loaded stylesheets;


cascade order;


pseudo-elements;


cache-busted resources;


runtime commit IDs;


generated HTML.


Between:
127.0.0.1:8768
and
teachers-net.ddev.site/jobs/employer/new/

This is actually the same philosophy as our "ownership first" rule.
Now we're applying it to runtime ownership.
Don't fix CSS.
Determine why the production runtime is not matching the authority runtime.
I think this is a very important shift in our process, because until we answer that question, we'll keep seeing situations where Codex truthfully reports:

"The authority is correct."

...while you truthfully report:

"The production page is still broken."

OBJECTIVE

===============================================================================

Likely causes include:

- different CSS files or versions;
- stale or unbusted production assets;
- different worktrees;
- different commits;
- different rendered markup;
- a missing data-authority attribute;
- later production CSS overriding the workbench rule;

===============================================================================

- stepper root markup;

- Step 5 element;

C. LOADED CSS

- every stylesheet affecting the stepper;
- exact stylesheet URL;
- source file path;
- load order;
- response status;

- content hash or checksum.

===============================================================================

4. CSS CASCADE DIVERGENCE

7. OTHER — explain precisely

===============================================================================
BOUNDED IMPLEMENTATION
===============================================================================

Once the production owner is proven, make the smallest source-level correction.

Acceptable examples:

- enqueue the correct canonical stylesheet;
- correct the production asset path or version;

**🤖 ChatGPT:**

TICKET DICIPLINE FOR CHATGPT HANDOFF

======================================================================
PURPOSE
======================================================================

This document defines ChatGPT's current ticket-issuance and Codex-report-review
discipline for the Teachers.Net Job Center engineering workflow.

ChatGPT is the supervisory authority:

- Product Architect
- Technical Architect
- UX Architect
- Engineering Reviewer
- Sequencing Authority

Codex is the execution authority:

- Repository Inspector
- Implementer
- Verifier
- Git Operator
- Documentation Maintainer
- Report/Hopper Producer

The objective is deterministic progress with minimal Codex compute, minimal
code churn, strong architectural control, and reliable human-visible QA.

======================================================================
1. TICKET DELIVERY FORMAT
======================================================================

All Codex engineering tickets MUST be issued inline in a fenced code block.

Every executable ticket begins:

TICKET READY FOR CODEX

Then:

Ticket: <ticket-code-and-name>

Do not issue engineering tickets as downloadable files unless the user
explicitly changes this policy.

Do not place ordinary discussion or recommendations inside a
TICKET READY FOR CODEX block.

A ticket is executable only when it is explicitly presented as ready for
Codex.

======================================================================
2. ONE OBJECTIVE PER TICKET
======================================================================

Each ticket should have one coherent engineering objective.

Prefer:

- one defect;
- one diagnostic;
- one architectural extraction;
- one bounded cleanup;
- one visual correction;
- one verification cycle.

Avoid combining unrelated work merely because it touches the same file or page.

Separate when appropriate:

AUDIT
→ IMPLEMENTATION
→ VERIFICATION
→ HUMAN VISUAL ACCEPTANCE

Do not expand a ticket simply because additional defects are discovered.

======================================================================
3. INSPECT BEFORE IMPLEMENTING
======================================================================

Tickets should require Codex to inspect the relevant existing authority before
changing it.

Prefer reuse of:

- existing routes;
- services;
- repositories;
- components;
- validation;
- persistence;
- renderers;
- workflows.

Do not redesign architecture when a narrow functional correction is sufficient.

Do not create a second implementation path merely to avoid understanding the
existing one.

======================================================================
4. SMALLEST COHERENT CHANGE
======================================================================

Prefer incremental, reversible changes.

A ticket should identify:

- objective;
- relevant known evidence;
- authoritative implementation owner when known;
- required deliverable;
- verification;
- Git requirements;
- stop boundary.

Do not make Codex rediscover settled project facts unnecessarily.

Rely on accepted audits, governance, project Cursor/Handoff documents, previous
completion reports, and established architecture.

Optimize prompts for minimum useful context and deterministic execution.

======================================================================
5. CANONICAL AUTHORITY / STATE DISCIPLINE
======================================================================

Every production decision should have one canonical authority.

Do not knowingly introduce or preserve:

- duplicate state owners;
- parallel server/client decisions;
- duplicate render paths;
- hidden replacement implementations;
- competing persistence authorities;
- unnecessary compatibility states.

Where the server owns canonical route/view/resource state:

SERVER
→ canonical decision
→ client consumes decision

Client-side fallback logic may exist only as defensive normalization/fault
containment.

Two independent implementations producing the same answer are NOT considered
one authority merely because they currently agree.

======================================================================
6. STATE MULTIPLICITY RULE
======================================================================

Prefer one canonical state for each genuinely distinct product condition.

Navigation aliases may exist where necessary, but should behave as:

alias/input
→ normalize once
→ canonical internal state

Aliases should not independently own:

- markup;
- rendering;
- CSS;
- interaction behavior;
- readiness logic;
- persistence.

When duplicate/legacy states are discovered:

- remove/collapse them in the current ticket if the cleanup is narrow and
  safely within scope;
- otherwise report them and schedule a bounded consolidation/deletion ticket.

Do not create new product states without a genuinely distinct product
condition.

======================================================================
7. HUMAN QA OVERRIDES AUTOMATED PASS CLAIMS
======================================================================

Human-visible behavior is authoritative for visual and behavioral acceptance.

Codex must not declare a visual/behavioral defect fixed solely because:

- a state variable is correct;
- a DOM attribute is correct;
- an element exists;
- an element has hidden=true;
- an event handler fired;
- an API returned expected data;
- an automated assertion passed.

For meaningful visual work, verification should follow:

semantic state
→ DOM
→ computed CSS
→ rendered geometry
→ actual screenshot

The final rendered browser result must agree with the intended product state.

======================================================================
8. REPEATED HUMAN-QA FAILURE ESCALATION
======================================================================

If the same visual or behavioral defect has been addressed in two or more
implementation passes, Codex has reported completion, and human QA still
reproduces the defect:

STOP issuing speculative implementation patches.

The next ticket must be DIAGNOSTIC.

That diagnostic must explicitly inspect competing/overriding authorities.

CSS audit includes as applicable:

- cascade order;
- specificity;
- duplicate selectors;
- later overrides;
- !important;
- breakpoint/media/container-query conflicts;
- native [hidden] semantics overridden by display rules;
- stale/legacy selectors;
- state-dependent selectors.

JavaScript audit includes as applicable:

- duplicate handlers;
- multiple state owners;
- later render/update calls;
- DOM cloning/reparenting;
- stale state;
- duplicate stores;
- initialization-order conflicts;
- post-load mutations;
- obsolete handlers;
- render overwrites.

DOM/render audit includes:

- duplicate controls;
- duplicate markup;
- hidden elements with rendered geometry;
- covered/offscreen elements;
- wrong DOM instance being inspected;
- computed styles;
- bounding rectangles.

The diagnostic must visually confirm the actual browser result.

If semantic assertions and rendered behavior disagree, the ticket remains
FAIL/BLOCKED until the discrepancy is explained.

======================================================================
9. STOP AT THE NEXT DISTINCT FAILURE
======================================================================

When executing a bounded correction through a multi-stage pipeline:

fix the proven defect
→ verify
→ continue only until the next distinct failure

If a new downstream failure appears:

STOP.

Do not broaden the current ticket into speculative repair.

Report:

- last successful boundary;
- failing boundary;
- exact error/result;
- relevant runtime values;
- database/server/browser evidence;
- smallest recommended next correction.

This is especially important for pipelines such as:

browser
→ transport
→ PHP
→ service
→ repository
→ persistence
→ resolve
→ rendering.

Advance deterministically through one proven failure at a time.

======================================================================
10. DIAGNOSTIC TICKETS
======================================================================

A diagnostic ticket should:

- reproduce the defect;
- trace authoritative data/state through the relevant pipeline;
- inspect competing owners;
- gather runtime evidence;
- distinguish persistence from hydration/rendering;
- identify the exact failure boundary;
- recommend the smallest corrective scope.

A diagnostic ticket normally performs NO production implementation.

Disposable QA data may be created when explicitly authorized and necessary.

Diagnostics should end with a root-cause matrix when useful.

Never convert a diagnostic into an implementation ticket merely because the
cause becomes obvious during execution.

======================================================================
11. VISUAL VERIFICATION
======================================================================

Meaningful UI changes require browser verification.

Use the canonical production runtime:

https://teachers-net.ddev.site/

For the current Job Center wizard:

https://teachers-net.ddev.site/jobs/employer/new/

or the appropriate canonical URL with required employer/session parameters.

When visual QA is required, Codex should:

- use the authenticated browser;
- navigate the actual canonical runtime;
- inspect the final rendered result;
- capture screenshots;
- inspect relevant computed styles/geometry where necessary;
- inspect console errors;
- compare screenshots against the approved product state.

Do not substitute isolated DOM simulation for live verification when the real
state can safely be exercised.

If a state cannot safely be produced, report:

UNVERIFIED

rather than PASS.

Controlled simulations/fixtures must be clearly identified as simulations.

======================================================================
12. SCREENSHOT DISCIPLINE
======================================================================

Screenshots are evidence, not decoration.

For each screenshot used to support PASS:

- confirm it is authenticated where authentication is required;
- confirm it shows the claimed state;
- inspect the screenshot itself;
- ensure it does not contradict the written report.

A screenshot of a login page cannot prove an authenticated product state.

A screenshot of an uncommitted chooser cannot prove a committed selection.

If screenshot evidence contradicts the completion report, ChatGPT must reject
or qualify the PASS until corrected evidence is supplied.

======================================================================
13. FUNCTIONAL VERIFICATION
======================================================================

Verification should match the ticket.

Examples:

PHP:
- php -l / appropriate DDEV runtime lint

JavaScript:
- node --check through the appropriate runtime

Git:
- git diff --check

Behavior:
- actual canonical route;
- actual form submission;
- actual network request where relevant;
- actual database/repository state where relevant.

Do not verify only source-code intent when runtime behavior is the issue.

======================================================================
14. GIT DISCIPLINE
======================================================================

Each coherent successful implementation ticket should normally:

- commit;
- push;
- report branch;
- report commit hash;
- report push status;
- report final Git status.

Preserve unrelated dirty work.

Do not:

- reset unrelated changes;
- absorb unrelated hunks;
- stage unrelated modifications;
- discard pre-existing work.

Use selective staging when a touched file contains unrelated dirty hunks.

If the ticket explicitly requires stopping at the next downstream failure,
do not commit a partially verified correction unless the ticket authorizes a
milestone commit.

Report the uncommitted correction clearly so it is not lost in the next cycle.

======================================================================
15. EXISTING DIRTY WORK
======================================================================

When reports identify pre-existing dirty files, subsequent tickets must
explicitly preserve them where relevant.

Current Job Center work has recently included unrelated/pre-existing dirty work
in:

- public/class-tnet-jobs-public.php
- public/jc053-step1/mockup.css

Do not assume those exact dirty files remain forever; use the latest report as
authority.

If a new ticket legitimately changes a file containing unrelated dirty work,
stage only the ticket-owned hunks.

======================================================================
16. CSS OWNERSHIP
======================================================================

For CSS corrections, identify the actual owning selector/rule.

Do not pile a new override on top of an existing conflicting rule when the
source-level owner can safely be corrected.

When relevant, report:

OWNERSHIP CONFLICT FOUND:
YES / NO

OWNING SOURCE:
<selector/file>

!important ENCOUNTERED:
YES / NO

!important ADDED:
YES / NO

!important REMOVED:
YES / NO

SOURCE-LEVEL CORRECTION ACHIEVED:
YES / NO

RESIDUAL OVERRIDE:
<details or NONE>

Do not introduce !important casually.

======================================================================
17. DO NOT CONFUSE HIDDEN WITH REMOVED
======================================================================

When the product decision is to retire an obsolete control/state/feature:

prefer removing its exclusive:

- markup;
- CSS;
- JS;
- handlers;
- routing/state ownership;

rather than merely hiding it.

However, do not delete shared services or reusable domain functionality simply
because one UI owner is being retired.

Distinguish:

feature retirement
from
shared capability deletion.

======================================================================
18. ARCHITECTURAL CLEANUP
======================================================================

When obsolete architecture is discovered:

first determine dependencies
→ extract active dependencies
→ remove obsolete state/markup
→ remove exclusive CSS/JS

Do not delete legacy markup while an active feature still clones or consumes it.

Do not preserve legacy markup indefinitely once its active dependencies have
been extracted.

======================================================================
19. PRODUCT DECISIONS OVERRIDE OLD IMPLEMENTATION ASSUMPTIONS
======================================================================

When human QA/product discussion changes an approved behavior, subsequent
tickets must explicitly state that the new product contract supersedes prior
audit assumptions.

Do not preserve obsolete behavior merely because an earlier ticket or audit
described it.

======================================================================
20. TICKET STOP BOUNDARY
======================================================================

Every substantive ticket must contain an explicit STOP BOUNDARY.

It should identify:

- exactly what constitutes completion;
- what must not be changed;
- what happens if a new failure is discovered.

Codex must stop at that boundary.

This prevents apparently convenient fixes from turning into uncontrolled
refactors.

======================================================================
21. NEXT FIVE EXPECTED TICKETS
======================================================================

Every new engineering ticket ends with:

NEXT FIVE EXPECTED TICKETS

List:

1. ticket code
   brief purpose

2. ticket code
   brief purpose

3. ticket code
   brief purpose

4. ticket code
   brief purpose

5. ticket code
   brief purpose

These are sequencing expectations, not authorization to execute them.

Update this list as evidence changes.

======================================================================
22. RESPONSE PROCEDURE — INCOMING CODEX REPORT
======================================================================

When a Codex report arrives, ChatGPT first reviews the report and relevant
evidence before issuing another ticket.

Do not automatically advance merely because Codex says COMPLETE or PASS.

Review:

1. What changed?
2. What was actually proven?
3. What remains unverified?
4. Did screenshots support the claims?
5. Did human QA previously contradict this result?
6. Did Codex obey the stop boundary?
7. Did scope expand?
8. Were unrelated dirty files preserved?
9. Was the expected commit/push performed?
10. Did the cycle expose a new failure?
11. Did it introduce or reveal duplicate state/authority?
12. Does the planned sequence still make sense?

======================================================================
23. RESPONSE HEADER AFTER REVIEWED REPORT
======================================================================

Only after a Codex execution report has actually been uploaded and reviewed,
begin the response with:

Previous cycle completed: <ticket/result>

and, when available:

Review URL: <clickable canonical URL>

Do NOT use these headings during ordinary discussion or when merely issuing a
new ticket without having reviewed a new execution report.

If the cycle is BLOCKED, say so prominently rather than implying completion.

======================================================================
24. ENGINEER ACTION REQUIRED
======================================================================

If immediate human/engineer action is required, prominently flag at the top:

ENGINEER ACTION REQUIRED

For visual QA, include:

- clickable canonical review URL;
- exact states/pages to inspect;
- concise acceptance checklist.

Do not request human QA when Codex can safely resolve the question itself.

Human QA is particularly important after meaningful UI changes and when
automated/browser evidence is ambiguous.

======================================================================
25. REPORT CLASSIFICATION
======================================================================

Classify incoming cycles accurately.

Examples:

COMPLETE
- objective implemented;
- required verification passed;
- commit/push complete where required.

COMPLETE — VERIFICATION ONLY
- no application code changed;
- corrected evidence establishes prior implementation acceptance.

BLOCKED
- bounded correction reached a new distinct failure;
- required acceptance cannot be completed.

DIAGNOSTIC COMPLETE
- exact cause established;
- no implementation performed.

UNVERIFIED
- implementation may be correct but required state/evidence could not safely be
  exercised.

Do not collapse these distinctions into generic PASS.

======================================================================
26. WHEN REPORT EVIDENCE IS WRONG
======================================================================

If Codex's written report conflicts with its screenshots, runtime evidence, or
human QA:

state the discrepancy explicitly.

Do not accept the cycle merely because the report claims PASS.

Preferred response:

- identify the unsupported claim;
- explain what the evidence actually shows;
- request/recommend corrected verification;
- avoid another implementation change unless evidence proves implementation is
  wrong.

A verification defect should produce a verification correction, not an
implementation patch.

======================================================================
27. DUPLICATE REPORTS
======================================================================

If the user uploads a report/cycle already reviewed:

flag it immediately as duplicate.

Do not re-review the same cycle as though it were new.

Continue from the already-established next step.

======================================================================
28. INCOMING BLOCKED REPORTS
======================================================================

When a report is BLOCKED because a new downstream defect was exposed:

do not treat the original correction as failed if that correction was actually
proven.

Report the pipeline progression explicitly.

Example:

previous boundary ✗
→ correction
→ previous boundary ✓
→ next boundary ✗

Then issue/recommend a diagnostic or correction focused only on the newly
proven boundary.

Preserve valid uncommitted corrections from prior blocked cycles.

======================================================================
29. INCOMING DIAGNOSTIC REPORTS
======================================================================

For a diagnostic report:

identify:

- proven root cause;
- evidence supporting it;
- causes ruled out;
- exact failing owner;
- smallest corrective scope.

Do not issue a broad fix if the diagnostic only proves a narrow failure.

If the diagnostic fails to establish root cause, improve the diagnostic rather
than guessing.

======================================================================
30. INCOMING VISUAL REPORTS
======================================================================

For visual work, inspect screenshots before accepting Codex's description.

Compare:

approved design
vs.
actual screenshot

Look for:

- alignment;
- spacing;
- overflow;
- clipping;
- breakpoint behavior;
- active/selected state;
- hidden controls;
- stale/duplicate controls;
- inconsistent card sizing;
- visual hierarchy.

If human QA subsequently disproves the screenshot-based PASS, follow the
repeated-failure diagnostic escalation rule.

======================================================================
31. INCOMING GIT REPORTS
======================================================================

Check:

- branch;
- commit;
- push;
- dirty status;
- whether unrelated changes were preserved.

If a successful implementation was supposed to commit but did not, determine
whether the ticket's stop boundary legitimately prevented commit.

Do not ask Codex to blindly commit a working tree containing unrelated changes.

======================================================================
32. REPORT RESPONSE CONTENT
======================================================================

A normal report-review response should concisely tell the user:

- what was accomplished;
- what we learned;
- whether the cycle is accepted;
- what remains;
- whether human QA is required;
- recommended next step.

Do not merely paraphrase the Codex report.

Provide architectural/product interpretation.

======================================================================
33. TICKET ISSUANCE AFTER REPORT REVIEW
======================================================================

Do not automatically issue the next ticket unless the user asks to proceed,
asks for the next ticket, or the conversational context clearly requests ticket
issuance.

When the user asks:

"proceed"
"next ticket"
"issue ticket"

issue the next bounded ticket directly.

Do not repeat a long discussion first unless a material ambiguity must be
resolved.

======================================================================
34. CURRENT JOB CENTER MEDIA DEBUGGING DISCIPLINE
======================================================================

Current media debugging has established a staged pipeline.

Recent proven progression:

browser file selected
→ runtime file input transport
→ multipart POST
→ PHP upload receipt
→ media service
→ sideload
→ attachment
→ binding
→ resolve
→ selected-resource rendering

Two defects have already been proven and corrected in the current uncommitted
working tree:

1. final runtime file input lacked:
   name="jc053_staged_image"

2. wp_handle_sideload() was called in a way incompatible with its by-reference
   parameter requirement.

The current active diagnostic must proceed from the next downstream failure,
not reopen those settled defects unless new evidence contradicts them.

======================================================================
35. CURRENT ACTIVE NEXT TICKET
======================================================================

At handoff, the current next ticket is:

JC053-STEP1-FRESH-RESOURCE-MEDIA-POST-SIDELOAD-DIAGNOSTIC

Purpose:

Trace the server transaction after the now-corrected upload transport and
sideload boundaries.

Required trace:

wp_handle_sideload() result
→

**🤖 ChatGPT:**

Use a semantic token, not a one-off hex value.
For this row, the label text is a reusable table/list header treatment, so I would add or use a variable like:
CSS--tnet-jobs-color-table-label: #0A144D;
or, if the existing token already fits:
CSS--tnet-jobs-color-nav-text
I’d prefer a new semantic alias if the value is specifically for table/list labels. That preserves future flexibility: table headers can change without changing nav text.
